# Item
## id
CON-FND-342EB8A955CCAE
## label
Localized suppurative inflammation (abscess, boil, carbuncle) is walled off by staphylococcal coagulase, while diffuse suppurative inflammation (cellulitis) spreads via streptococcal fibrinolysin and hyaluronidase
## canonical_key
suppurativeinflammation.types.cellulitis-vs-abscess-vs-pseudomembranous
## aliases
Localized vs diffuse suppurative inflammation
Abscess vs cellulitis
Pseudomembranous inflammation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Suppurative inflammation is acute inflammation with substantial purulent exudate (pus) — neutrophils, pus cells, necrotic tissue and edema fluid — caused by pyogenic organisms. It splits into two patterns by the causative organism's own enzymes. Localized suppurative inflammation (abscess, boil/furuncle, carbuncle) is commonly caused by Staphylococcus aureus, which secretes coagulase; coagulase converts fibrinogen to fibrin and walls the pus off into a discrete cavity. Diffuse suppurative inflammation — cellulitis in loose tissues (subcutaneous tissue, orbit, scrotum), acute diffuse suppurative appendicitis, septic peritonitis — is commonly caused by Streptococcus pyogenes (haemolyticus), whose fibrinolysin breaks down fibrin (so nothing walls the pus off) and whose hyaluronidase is a spreading factor; its pus is thin and blood-stained because streptococcal toxins haemolyse red cells. Pseudomembranous inflammation is a distinct, severe pattern on mucous membranes: a necrotizing bacterial toxin (diphtheria bacilli in the throat/larynx, shigella in the colon causing bacillary dysentery) kills the surface epithelium, and the mucosa is covered by a false membrane of precipitated fibrin, necrotic epithelium and leukocytes — it is not simply a mild form of acute suppurative inflammation.
## explicit_objective
Distinguish localized from diffuse suppurative inflammation by causative organism and its virulence enzyme, and separate pseudomembranous inflammation as its own toxin-mediated mucosal pattern rather than a mild variant of suppuration.
## pitfalls
Calling pseudomembranous inflammation a 'mild form' of acute suppurative inflammation — it is a severe, toxin-mediated necrotizing pattern (diphtheria, bacillary dysentery), not suppurative at all in mechanism. Assigning coagulase to streptococci and fibrinolysin to staphylococci — it is the reverse: staphylococcal coagulase walls infection off (favouring an abscess), streptococcal fibrinolysin dissolves the wall and lets infection spread diffusely (favouring cellulitis).

# Item
## id
CON-FND-9853F779478D1A
## label
Granulation tissue is proliferating capillaries and fibroblasts, moist red and easily bleeding, formed early in healing by fibrosis
## canonical_key
granulationtissue.composition.fibroblasts-and-new-capillaries
## aliases
Granulation tissue composition
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Granulation tissue is the early stage of repair by fibrosis: it is formed of proliferating capillaries and fibroblasts (some transformed into myofibroblasts). The name derives from the granular appearance of the newly formed capillaries at the base of a healing skin ulcer. Granulation tissue is moist, red and bleeds easily — it is neither avascular nor dry. It forms through angiogenesis (new capillary sprouting from existing venules, driven mainly by VEGF and FGF) together with fibroblast migration and proliferation (driven mainly by PDGF, FGF-2 and TGF-beta, largely from macrophages) and deposition of extracellular matrix. As repair continues, granulation tissue matures: proliferating endothelial cells and fibroblasts decrease in number while collagen type I deposition increases, giving the eventual scar its tensile strength.
## explicit_objective
State what granulation tissue is composed of, what it looks like grossly, and how it differs from the mature scar it becomes.
## pitfalls
Describing granulation tissue as avascular or dry scar tissue — that description fits the late, remodelled scar, not granulation tissue itself, which is vascular, moist and red because it is still actively forming new capillaries.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-434E7A6D0A5BF3
## label
Healing occurs by regeneration when injury affects only labile or minimally damaged stable parenchymal cells, and by fibrosis when it destroys the tissue framework or permanent cells; overdone or defective repair produces its own complications
## canonical_key
woundhealing.regeneration-vs-fibrosis.liver-cirrhosis-and-complications
## aliases
Regeneration vs fibrosis (healing)
Liver cirrhosis mechanism
Complications of wound healing
Marjolin ulcer
Keloid
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Healing is replacement of injured tissue by new tissue, occurring by regeneration (same cell type, restoring normal structure) or fibrosis (scar formation). Regeneration follows damage to rapidly dividing labile cells (surface epithelium, hematopoietic cells) or minimal damage confined to the parenchymal cells of a stable organ such as the liver, which has excellent regenerative capacity despite replicating slowly. Fibrosis follows damage to permanent, non-dividing cells (cardiac muscle, nerve cells) or severe damage that destroys both the parenchymal cells and the supporting tissue framework — in the liver, injury confined to hepatocytes is followed by regeneration, but when chronic injury destroys both hepatocytes and the connective-tissue architecture, the liver cells regenerate as disorganised nodules surrounded by fibrous scarring: cirrhosis. Endometrial lining, being labile surface epithelium, also regenerates. Wound healing itself can produce its own complications distinct from the underlying disease: cosmetic deformity from extensive scarring, contracture (scar shortening limiting joint movement), keloid (genetically determined overproduction of fibroblasts and collagen after dermal injury — overdone repair), implantation epidermal cyst (dermoid cyst) from epidermal cells trapped in the dermis during regeneration, chronic ulcer/sinus/fistula from defective repair with persistent infection, and, rarely, carcinoma arising from the edge of a chronic ulcer or burn scar (Marjolin's ulcer).
## explicit_objective
State which tissue and injury pattern predicts regeneration versus fibrosis, explain why liver injury to the framework (not just hepatocytes) produces cirrhosis, and list the recognised complications of wound healing.
## pitfalls
Assuming any liver injury heals by regeneration because the liver famously regenerates — regeneration only restores normal architecture when the connective-tissue framework survives; once the framework itself is destroyed, regenerating hepatocyte nodules are walled off by fibrous scar (cirrhosis), which is fibrosis, not true regeneration. Listing cosmetic deformity as an exception to normal complications rather than recognising it, along with carcinoma, dermoid cyst and chronic sinus, as one of the genuinely recognised complications of wound healing.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-685D424733A1DB
## label
Lymphocytes, plasma cells and macrophages are the key mononuclear cells of chronic inflammation, distinct from the neutrophil-dominated picture of acute inflammation
## canonical_key
chronicinflammation.cells.lymphocytes-plasmacells-macrophages
## aliases
Cells of chronic inflammation
Mononuclear inflammatory cells
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Chronic inflammation is inflammation of prolonged duration (weeks to years) in which continuing inflammation, tissue injury and healing by fibrosis proceed together — arising from persistent infection (e.g. Mycobacterium tuberculosis, Treponema pallidum), immune-mediated/autoimmune disease, or prolonged exposure to toxic agents such as silica. Its pathologic hallmark is infiltration by mononuclear cells rather than neutrophils: blood monocytes migrate in and become macrophages (the key cell of chronic inflammation, activated by microbial products or T-cell cytokines to proliferate, persist, and secrete proteolytic enzymes and growth factors that drive both tissue damage and fibrosis), and B lymphocytes that transform into antibody-secreting plasma cells. T lymphocytes are also present, activated to secrete cytokines. There is minimal edema fluid compared with acute inflammation, and ongoing tissue destruction is matched by attempted healing through fibrosis.
## explicit_objective
Name the key cell types of chronic inflammation and state why macrophages, lymphocytes and plasma cells (not neutrophils) dominate the picture.
## pitfalls
Naming eosinophils or neutrophils as the defining cells of chronic inflammation — eosinophils are specific to parasitic/allergic settings and neutrophils are the hallmark of acute inflammation (though they can persist in 'acute on chronic' lesions); the key triad the department book names for chronic inflammation itself is lymphocytes, plasma cells and macrophages.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-D798D9598473F6
## label
A sinus is a blind-ended tract from a deep focus to the surface; a fistula is a tract that connects two surfaces or hollow organs
## canonical_key
sinus.definition.blind-ended-tract-vs-fistula
## aliases
Sinus vs fistula
Abscess complications (sinus, fistula, ulcer)
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A sinus is a blind-ended tract between a deep-seated focus (typically an abscess) and the body surface — for example a peritoneal abscess tracking to a sinus on the abdominal wall. A fistula is a double-ended tract communicating between two surfaces or hollow organs, formed when a deep abscess evacuates and its tract connects two epithelialised surfaces (an ano-rectal fistula complicating a perianal abscess is the worked example). Both are complications of evacuation and defective healing of a suppurative focus and, if the underlying infection persists, both become chronic. An ulcer, by contrast, is a local surface defect (skin or mucosa) from separation of inflammatory necrotic tissue — it has only one open end and does not tunnel to a deep focus, which is what distinguishes it from a sinus.
## explicit_objective
Distinguish a sinus (blind-ended, one open end) from a fistula (double-ended, connects two surfaces) and from an ulcer (surface defect, no tract).
## pitfalls
Confusing a sinus with a fistula by the number of open ends — a sinus has exactly one open end (blind internally), a fistula has two. Confusing either with an ulcer, which is a surface defect with no tract at all.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-B56A1400847F2D
## label
Serum tumor markers are organ- or tumor-type-specific: PSA for prostate, calcitonin for medullary thyroid carcinoma, AFP for hepatocellular carcinoma, cytokeratin for carcinomas generally
## canonical_key
tumormarker.specificity.psa-cytokeratin-afp-calcitonin
## aliases
Tumor markers table
PSA
Alpha-fetoprotein
Carcinoembryonic antigen
Immunohistochemical markers
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Serum and immunohistochemical tumor markers cannot make a definitive diagnosis of cancer on their own, but they help detect cancer and monitor treatment response or recurrence. By hormone: human chorionic gonadotrophin marks trophoblastic tumors, calcitonin marks medullary carcinoma of the thyroid. By oncofetal antigen: alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) marks hepatocellular carcinoma, carcinoembryonic antigen (CEA) marks carcinomas of colon, pancreas, lung, stomach and breast. By antigen: prostatic specific antigen (PSA) marks prostate cancer, immunoglobulins mark multiple myeloma. By mucin/glycoprotein: CA-125 marks ovarian cancer, CA-19-9 marks colon and pancreatic cancer. Separately, immunohistochemistry uses antibodies to identify the tissue of origin of poorly differentiated tumors: leucocyte common antigen (LCA) for lymphomas, cytokeratins for carcinomas, vimentin for sarcomas, and S100 protein for Schwann-cell tumors and malignant melanoma.
## explicit_objective
Match each named tumor marker (PSA, calcitonin, AFP, CEA, cytokeratin, vimentin, LCA, S100) to the tumor type it identifies.
## pitfalls
Assigning a marker to the wrong tissue class — cytokeratin marks epithelial/carcinoma differentiation, not squamous cell carcinoma specifically; calcitonin is specific to medullary (not papillary or follicular) thyroid carcinoma; AFP is hepatocellular carcinoma, not a general oncofetal marker for all GIT cancers (that is CEA's role).

# Item
## id
CON-FND-D1D48A5564E978
## label
Aflatoxin, a chemical carcinogen from Aspergillus flavus, causes hepatocellular carcinoma
## canonical_key
carcinogen.chemical.aflatoxin-hepatocellular-carcinoma
## aliases
Aflatoxin
Chemical carcinogens
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Chemical carcinogens are mostly mutagenic, damaging proto-oncogenes and tumor suppressor genes to initiate malignant transformation. The department book's named examples each pair a chemical with the cancer it causes: aromatic hydrocarbons (3,4-benzpyrene, from cigarette smoke) cause lung cancer; aromatic amines (beta-naphthylamine) cause bladder cancer; asbestos causes mesothelioma; vinyl chloride causes hepatic angiosarcoma; arsenic causes skin cancer; and aflatoxins — produced by the fungus Aspergillus flavus, a food (grain/nut) contaminant rather than a synthetic industrial chemical — cause hepatic (hepatocellular) carcinoma.
## explicit_objective
Name the chemical carcinogen produced by Aspergillus flavus and the cancer it causes, and place it correctly among the department book’s other named chemical carcinogen-cancer pairs.
## pitfalls
Confusing aflatoxin (a fungal food contaminant causing hepatocellular carcinoma) with hepatitis B virus, which is the department book's separate viral cause of hepatocellular carcinoma — both target the liver but by entirely different mechanisms and are listed under different carcinogen categories.

## id
CON-FND-4BA8E4B11685CA
## label
Epstein-Barr virus causes Burkitt’s lymphoma, nasopharyngeal carcinoma and other B-cell lymphomas including some Hodgkin lymphoma
## canonical_key
carcinogen.viral.ebv-burkitt-lymphoma
## aliases
Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) oncogenesis
Viral carcinogens
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Oncogenic viruses cause cancer either by integrating a viral oncogene into host DNA or by producing proteins that inactivate tumor suppressor gene products. Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) is implicated in Burkitt's lymphoma, nasopharyngeal carcinoma, other B-cell lymphomas, and some cases of Hodgkin's lymphoma. This sits alongside the department book's other named oncogenic viruses: hepatitis B virus causes hepatocellular carcinoma, human papilloma virus causes cervical carcinoma and some skin carcinomas, and HTLV-1 causes T-cell leukemia/lymphoma.
## explicit_objective
Name the tumor(s) Epstein-Barr virus causes and place EBV correctly among the department book’s other oncogenic viruses and the cancers each one causes.
## pitfalls
Assigning EBV to a carcinoma it does not cause in this teaching (cervical carcinoma is HPV's tumor, hepatocellular carcinoma is hepatitis B's) rather than to Burkitt's lymphoma and nasopharyngeal carcinoma, its two named associations.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-C9F89672B04076
## label
Proto-oncogenes drive proliferation and become oncogenes on gain-of-function mutation; tumor suppressor genes ("governors" like RB, "guardians" like TP53) restrain proliferation or sense DNA damage
## canonical_key
oncogene.classification.proto-oncogene-vs-tumorsuppressor-vs-guardian
## aliases
Proto-oncogenes
Oncogenes
Tumor suppressor genes
TP53 guardian of the genome
RB gene
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Proto-oncogenes are normal cellular genes whose products drive cell proliferation; a gain-of-function change (point mutation, chromosomal translocation, or gene amplification) converts a proto-oncogene into a cancer-causing oncogene. The four main categories of oncogene the department book names are genes producing growth factors, genes producing growth factor receptors (e.g. erbB2/HER2), genes encoding signal transduction proteins (e.g. RAS), and genes encoding DNA transcription factors (e.g. MYC) — genes that stop cell proliferation are, by definition, not oncogenes. Tumor suppressor genes fall into two groups: 'governors', classic tumor suppressors whose products normally stop cell proliferation (retinoblastoma gene, RB — its mutation permits uncontrolled proliferation), and 'guardians', which sense genomic damage and arrest proliferation for DNA repair or trigger apoptosis if the damage cannot be repaired. TP53 is the prototypic guardian, the 'guardian of the genome' — its mutation does not directly transform cells (loss of guardian function has no direct effect on proliferation or apoptosis by itself), but it permits and accelerates the accumulation of further mutations in oncogenes and other tumor suppressor genes that do transform the cell.
## explicit_objective
Classify a named gene as proto-oncogene/oncogene or as a governor/guardian tumor suppressor gene, and state which category TP53 belongs to and why its mutation is indirect.
## pitfalls
Calling TP53 a governor rather than the prototypic guardian — RB is the department book's worked governor example (stops proliferation directly), TP53 is the worked guardian example (senses damage, and its mutation only accelerates further mutation rather than directly driving proliferation). Listing 'genes which encode products that stop cell proliferation' as a category of oncogene — that description is the tumor suppressor gene's role, the functional opposite of what an oncogene does.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-31B9E6BCCD93E4
## label
Hypertrophy is increased cell size without division, hyperplasia is increased cell number by division, and both are reversible adaptive responses unlike neoplasia
## canonical_key
disorderedgrowth.definitions.hyperplasia-hypertrophy-atrophy-dysplasia
## aliases
Hypertrophy definition
Hyperplasia definition
Cellular adaptation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Cells adapt to a changing environment by altered growth. Increased growth occurs as hypertrophy (increased cell size without cell replication — increased organ weight from synthesis of additional intracellular structural components, e.g. left ventricular hypertrophy from hypertension), hyperplasia (increased cell number by cell division, e.g. hormonal hyperplasia of the breast at puberty), or a combination of both. Decreased growth is atrophy (decreased organ size and weight after the organ reached adult size, due to decreased cell size — e.g. disuse atrophy after prolonged immobilisation, or the generalised atrophy of advanced malignancy's cachexia). Altered growth and differentiation covers metaplasia and dysplasia. Unlike neoplasia, adaptive hyperplasia is excited by a stimulus and is reversible — it stops and regresses once the stimulus is withdrawn, and the proliferated cells remain normal in shape; neoplasia proliferates independent of any ongoing stimulus, is irreversible, and its cells are abnormally shaped.
## explicit_objective
Define hypertrophy, hyperplasia and atrophy by their cellular mechanism (size vs number vs both), and state the two features (reversibility, stimulus-dependence) that separate reversible hyperplasia from irreversible neoplastic proliferation.
## pitfalls
Defining hypertrophy as an increase in cell number — that is hyperplasia's mechanism; hypertrophy is specifically an increase in the size of existing cells without division. Treating pathological hyperplasia as inherently as dangerous as neoplasia — hyperplasia remains reversible and stimulus-dependent, which is exactly what distinguishes it from the autonomous, irreversible proliferation of a true neoplasm.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-7F1B5B78F0DF85
## label
Squamous, intestinal and apocrine metaplasia are recognised precancerous conditions at several named sites, but not every case of squamous metaplasia is classed as premalignant in the department book’s own exception list
## canonical_key
metaplasia.precancerous-potential.sites-and-exceptions
## aliases
Metaplasia as a precancerous condition
Preneoplastic lesions
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Metaplasia — replacement of one mature differentiated cell type by another, usually an adaptive response to chronic injury in which the new epithelium better resists it — is often associated with increased malignancy risk, because it is achieved by reprogramming tissue stem cells rather than a stable, permanent adaptation. The department book's list of non-hereditary preneoplastic disorders explicitly names hyperplastic, metaplastic and dysplastic lesions as precancerous — leukoplakia, endometrial hyperplasia, mammary hyperplasia and cervical dysplasia are its worked examples — and separately names bilharziasis of the urinary bladder (squamous metaplasia of transitional epithelium) among its chronic inflammatory precancerous conditions. Squamous cell carcinoma of the urinary bladder specifically arising on top of squamous metaplasia due to bilharziasis is stated directly under squamous cell carcinoma's own predisposing factors. Bronchial squamous metaplasia in smokers is likewise linked to squamous cell carcinoma risk. Apocrine metaplasia in fibrocystic disease of the breast, by contrast, is not on the department book's list of premalignant metaplastic changes — of the four sites tested (bladder, bronchus, gastric intestinal metaplasia, and apocrine breast metaplasia), apocrine metaplasia is the one the source text does not carry a precancerous association for.
## explicit_objective
State which named metaplastic changes (bladder squamous metaplasia in bilharziasis, bronchial squamous metaplasia in smokers, gastric intestinal metaplasia) the department book links to malignancy risk, and identify apocrine breast metaplasia as the site it does not.
## pitfalls
Assuming every metaplastic change is equally premalignant — the department book links squamous, intestinal and glandular (Barrett's) metaplasia to specific cancers at specific sites, but does not carry the same premalignant association for apocrine metaplasia in fibrocystic breast disease.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-AA08F4558D39DB
## label
Malignant cachexia — marked loss of fat and lean body mass, weakness and anemia — results from cytokines produced by the tumor and host, not from the tumor’s own nutritional demands
## canonical_key
cachexia.malignant.mechanism-and-features
## aliases
Cancer cachexia mechanism
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Malignant cachexia is a marked decrease in body fat and lean body mass, with weakness, anorexia and anemia, seen in advanced cancer. There is some correlation between tumor size/extent of spread and the degree of cachexia, but cachexia does not result from the tumor's nutritional demands on the host — the tumor is not simply 'consuming' the patient's nutrition. Instead, cachexia results from the action of soluble factors — cytokines such as TNF, produced by both the tumor and host macrophages — acting systemically on metabolism.
## explicit_objective
State the mechanism of malignant cachexia and distinguish it from a simple nutritional-demand explanation.
## pitfalls
Attributing cachexia to the tumor's nutritional demands outstripping the host's intake — the department book states explicitly that cachexia does not result from the tumor's nutritional demands, but from cytokine action (produced by the tumor and by host macrophages, e.g. TNF) on host metabolism.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-B7FE536D59F695
## label
Leukoplakia is a hyperkeratotic white mucosal plaque that is itself a precursor lesion to squamous cell carcinoma, not carcinoma in situ
## canonical_key
leukoplakia.definition.keratinization-not-cis
## aliases
Leukoplakia definition
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Leukoplakia is a hyperkeratotic (white) plaque or patch of mucosa, exhibiting clonality, and representing a precursor lesion to squamous cell carcinoma — it is not itself squamous cell carcinoma or carcinoma in situ, though roughly 40% of leukoplakias show keratinizing dysplasia. It arises at sites of chronic irritation in the oral cavity or vulva, and may also develop after squamous metaplasia in the urinary bladder or bronchus. Grossly it appears as thick, irregular white patches; microscopically as thickened squamous epithelium with keratinization and subepithelial chronic inflammation. The department book's non-hereditary preneoplastic list names leukoplakia directly as an example of a hyperplastic/metaplastic/dysplastic precancerous lesion.
## explicit_objective
Define leukoplakia by its gross and microscopic appearance and state its relationship to squamous cell carcinoma as a precursor lesion rather than an established malignancy.
## pitfalls
Equating leukoplakia with carcinoma in situ or with squamous epithelial keratinization in general — leukoplakia is a specific clinical white plaque that is a precursor lesion (about 40% show dysplasia), a step before, not equivalent to, carcinoma in situ.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-D75B95517F50CF
## label
Anaplasia (cellular and nuclear pleomorphism, loss of polarity, abnormal mitoses) grades a malignant tumor’s differentiation, and worse differentiation means faster growth and more anaplasia, not less
## canonical_key
tumordifferentiation.anaplasia-grading.benign-vs-malignant-features
## aliases
Anaplasia
Tumor grading (grade I-IV)
Histologic differentiation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Histologic differentiation is the degree to which a tumor's structural pattern resembles the normal tissue it arose from. Malignant cells show grades of cellular atypia called anaplasia — cellular and nuclear pleomorphism, nuclear hyperchromatism with an increased nucleocytoplasmic ratio, prominent nucleoli, abundant and abnormal mitoses, and tumor giant cells — and, in addition to these cytologic abnormalities, anaplastic cells lose their normal architectural orientation (polarity), growing in sheets rather than recognisable glands or layers. Carcinomas are graded well differentiated (grade I), moderately differentiated (grade II), poorly differentiated (grade III) or undifferentiated (grade IV, sometimes termed anaplastic carcinoma). Better-differentiated tumors show less marked anaplasia and grow more slowly; undifferentiated tumors show the greatest cellular anaplasia and grow the fastest — grade and behaviour move together, they do not run in opposite directions. Sarcomas, too, may be graded across this same well-to-undifferentiated spectrum.
## explicit_objective
State how tumor grade (I-IV) relates to degree of anaplasia and growth rate, and recognise that benign tumors, not malignant ones, show normal cellular polarity.
## pitfalls
Assuming better-differentiated (lower-grade) tumors show greater anaplasia or grow faster — the relationship runs the other way: better differentiation means less anaplasia and slower growth, while undifferentiated (grade IV) tumors show the most anaplasia and grow fastest. Attributing normal cellular polarity to anaplastic (malignant) cells — loss of polarity is a defining feature of anaplasia, not a benign-tumor feature misapplied to malignant ones.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-772C8ED8D14A6D
## label
Macrophages, derived from blood monocytes and capable of phagocytosis, normally die or wander off into lymphatics once the inflammatory stimulus is eliminated, but persist and accumulate in chronic inflammation
## canonical_key
macrophage.fate.postinflammation-death-and-migration
## aliases
Macrophage fate after inflammation resolves
Macrophage origin
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Macrophages are derived from blood monocytes, which leave the vessels at the site of inflammation, transform into macrophages, and become active, motile and phagocytic. In most acute inflammation, macrophages play a lesser phagocytic role than neutrophils, not predominating until later stages when neutrophil numbers have fallen and macrophage numbers have risen through local proliferation; besides phagocytosis, they secrete growth factors important for repair. Once the initiating stimulus is eliminated and the inflammatory reaction ends, macrophages eventually die or wander off into the lymphatics — they do not, as a rule, remain and continue to accumulate. In chronic inflammatory sites, by contrast, macrophage accumulation persists because of continued recruitment from the blood and local proliferation, and interferon-gamma can fuse activated macrophages into large multinucleate giant cells.
## explicit_objective
State what happens to macrophages once an acute inflammatory reaction resolves, and contrast this with why they persist and accumulate in chronic inflammation.
## pitfalls
Assuming macrophages always persist and continue accumulating wherever they are recruited — in a resolved acute inflammatory reaction they die or wander off into the lymphatics once the stimulus is gone; persistence and accumulation is specifically a feature of ongoing chronic inflammation, driven by continued recruitment and local proliferation.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-759929134FF959
## label
Leukocyte recruitment in acute inflammation follows a fixed sequence — margination and rolling (selectins), pavementing/adhesion (integrins), then emigration and chemotaxis — with neutrophils arriving before monocytes
## canonical_key
acuteinflammation.cellularevents.margination-rolling-integrin-emigration-sequence
## aliases
Margination and rolling
Pavementing
Cellular events of acute inflammation
Leukocyte emigration sequence
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Leukocyte recruitment in acute inflammation follows an ordered sequence. First, slowed blood flow at the site of inflammation lets neutrophils move out of the normal central (axial) stream into the peripheral plasmatic zone near the endothelium — margination. Loosely adherent leukocytes then tumble along the activated endothelium, mediated by the selectin family of adhesion molecules — rolling. Firmer adhesion follows — pavementing — mediated by integrins on the leukocyte surface binding their endothelial ligands. Leukocytes then emigrate through the vessel wall, chiefly at venules, by squeezing between endothelial cells, driven by chemokines in the extravascular tissue; after crossing the endothelium they secrete collagenases to cross the basement membrane. Finally, having left the blood, leukocytes move toward the site of injury along a chemical gradient — chemotaxis, driven by bacterial products, chemokines, complement component C5a, and leukotriene B4. Neutrophils accumulate first in the extravascular space, followed by monocytes — not the reverse — and leucocyte emigration is not followed by leucopenia (in fact acute inflammation typically produces leukocytosis, driven by cytokine-stimulated colony-stimulating factors).
## explicit_objective
Sequence the cellular events of acute inflammation (margination and rolling via selectins, pavementing via integrins, emigration, chemotaxis) and state that neutrophils precede monocytes into the extravascular space.
## pitfalls
Reversing the order of neutrophil and monocyte arrival, or the mediators of rolling versus firm adhesion — selectins mediate the loose, tumbling rolling step; integrins mediate the firmer pavementing/adhesion step that follows it. Assuming leukocyte emigration causes leucopenia — emigration depletes the local marginating pool, not the total circulating count, and the systemic response to acute inflammation is typically leukocytosis, not leukopenia.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-AE5841979FCFAE
## label
Sarcomas form large, bulky, expansile masses and are much less common than carcinomas
## canonical_key
sarcoma.general-biology.vs-carcinoma
## aliases
Carcinoma vs sarcoma general features
Sarcoma gross features
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
A sarcoma is a malignant tumour of mesenchyme (connective tissue, fat, muscle, bone, cartilage, vessels), in contrast to a carcinoma, which is a malignant tumour of epithelium. Sarcomas are much less common than carcinomas, tend to occur in adolescents and young adults rather than the over-40s typical of carcinoma, and grow faster. Because of this rapid, expansile growth they form large, bulky, fleshy (sarc = flesh in Greek) masses with a pink tinge from rich vascularity, softer consistency, greater cellular anaplasia and less cell-to-cell cohesion than carcinoma, and they spread early and predominantly by the bloodstream rather than by lymphatics.
## explicit_objective
Distinguish the general gross and behavioural features of sarcoma (large bulky mass, rapid growth, blood-borne spread, rarity relative to carcinoma) from those of carcinoma.
## pitfalls
Assuming sarcoma is the more common malignancy, or that it spreads mainly by lymphatics — the department book's comparison table gives carcinoma both the higher frequency and the lymphatic-first spread pattern, with sarcoma spreading early by blood.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-86BABEB81453F4
## label
Edema arises from increased hydrostatic pressure, reduced plasma osmotic pressure, lymphatic obstruction, sodium retention or inflammation
## canonical_key
edema.causes.classification
## aliases
Causes of edema
Generalized vs localized edema
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Edema is the pathological accumulation of excess fluid (transudate or exudate) in the interstitial tissue spaces and serous sacs. The department book lists five categories of cause: increased hydrostatic pressure (impaired venous return as in congestive heart failure, or venous obstruction by thrombus or external compression such as pregnancy); reduced plasma osmotic pressure from hypoproteinemia (nephrotic syndrome, liver cirrhosis, malnutrition); lymphatic obstruction (congenital hypoplasia, inflammatory lymphadenitis or filariasis, neoplastic permeation, or post-surgical/post-irradiation fibrosis); sodium retention (excessive salt intake with renal insufficiency, or renal hypoperfusion); and inflammation, through the formation of an acute inflammatory exudate. Decreased hydrostatic pressure is not among these causes, since a fall in hydrostatic pressure works against, rather than promotes, fluid leaving the vasculature.
## explicit_objective
List the five categories of cause of edema (hydrostatic, osmotic, lymphatic, sodium retention, inflammatory) and recognise which changes in each direction actually promote edema.
## pitfalls
Assuming any change in hydrostatic pressure causes edema — it is specifically an increase, from impaired venous return or venous obstruction, that does so; a decrease works in the opposite direction.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-70B16C02F32AA5
## label
Congestion is a passive process caused by impaired blood outflow, distinct from the active vasodilatation of hyperemia
## canonical_key
congestion.definition.systemic-vs-localized
## aliases
Hyperemia vs congestion
Systemic and localized congestion
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Congestion is a passive process caused by impaired blood outflow from a tissue, resulting in increased blood content; it may be systemic (generalized), as in heart failure, or localized, as with occlusion or external pressure on a vein, and either form may be acute or chronic. This is mechanistically distinct from hyperemia, an active process of increased blood inflow due to active vasodilatation of arterioles and capillaries, physiological in exercising skeletal muscle or pathological at sites of inflammation. In acute congestion the vessels are distended and the organ is grossly red, usually with interstitial edema; in chronic congestion, gradual congestion produces edema, focal hemorrhages and hemosiderin-laden macrophages, and the associated chronic hypoxia can cause ischemic injury and scarring, as seen in chronic venous congestion of the lung, liver (nutmeg liver) and spleen.
## explicit_objective
Define congestion as a passive process of impaired venous outflow and contrast it with the active vasodilatation of hyperemia, including the acute vs chronic morphological picture.
## pitfalls
Confusing congestion (passive, outflow-blocked) with hyperemia (active, inflow-driven) — both make a tissue look red and blood-engorged, but the underlying mechanism, and therefore the clinical implication, is opposite.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-7805BA779D3A2D
## label
Virchow's triad names endothelial injury, altered blood flow and hypercoagulability as the three causes of thrombosis
## canonical_key
thrombosis.etiology.virchows-triad
## aliases
Virchow triad
Etiology of thrombosis
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The department book names three primary factors in thrombus formation, Virchow's triad: endothelial injury, the dominant cause, from endocarditis, ulcerated atherosclerotic plaque, hemodynamic stress (hypertension, turbulent flow) or noxious agents (endotoxin, radiation, hypercholesterolemia, cigarette smoke); alterations in normal blood flow, where stasis and turbulence disrupt laminar flow, bring platelets into contact with endothelium, prevent dilution of activated clotting factors, retard the inflow of clotting inhibitors, and promote endothelial activation; and hypercoagulability, any alteration of the coagulation pathways predisposing to thrombosis, whether heritable (antithrombin III, protein C or protein S deficiency) or acquired (oral contraceptives, pregnancy, postoperative platelet rise, procoagulant products from certain malignancies).
## explicit_objective
Name the three elements of Virchow's triad exactly as the department book states them, without inflating the list with a restated element.
## pitfalls
Treating 'vascular wall injury' as a fourth, distinct member of the triad alongside endothelial injury — the book names only the three factors above, and vascular wall injury is not a separate listed element.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-2F4E0092DCAF16
## label
Mural thrombi form on cardiac chamber and aortic walls without occluding the lumen, and venous thrombi propagate by repeated clotting at the next open tributary
## canonical_key
thrombus.sites-and-propagation.mural-and-venous
## aliases
Mural thrombus
Thrombus propagation
Sites and types of thrombi
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Mural thrombi form on the walls of the cardiac chambers and the aorta and, as a rule, do not occlude the lumen. Arterial thrombi usually occur over ruptured atherosclerotic plaques, generally occlude the artery and adhere tightly, and seldom embolize (except in the aorta). Vegetations are thrombi on cardiac valves. Venous thrombi almost always occlude the vein and embolize easily, because veins are thin-walled, superficial and slow-flowing; when a venous thrombus occludes a vein completely, the proximal column of blood clots to the next tributary, another thrombus forms opposite that tributary where blood is still moving, and once it too occludes the lumen the cycle repeats — propagation — which can extend all the way to the heart.
## explicit_objective
Place mural thrombi in the cardiac chambers and aorta, distinguish them from valvular vegetations and occlusive arterial or venous thrombi, and explain propagation as a venous phenomenon of repeated occlusion at successive tributaries.
## pitfalls
Applying propagation to arterial thrombi — the book's mechanism (clot proximal to a tributary, new clot opposite it, repeating) is described specifically for veins, which occlude completely, unlike most arterial thrombi, which adhere tightly without necessarily occluding.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-0FC5FDF1C3874D
## label
Deep leg veins are the major source of pulmonary emboli, which travel from a systemic vein or the right heart to the lungs
## canonical_key
embolism.pulmonary.source-and-effects
## aliases
Source of pulmonary emboli
Pulmonary thromboembolism
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The department book states that most pulmonary emboli come from the deep leg veins, and may also arise from thrombi in the iliac and pelvic veins or the right side of the heart, following the general course-of-emboli rule that emboli from a systemic vein or right heart impact in the lungs. Their effect depends on size: small emboli (60-80% of pulmonary emboli) are clinically silent; a medium embolus in healthy lungs has no effect because of the dual bronchial and pulmonary blood supply, but in a lung already chronically congested it causes hemorrhagic infarction; a big embolus occluding the pulmonary trunk or a main branch releases serotonin from platelets, causing bilateral pulmonary artery vasoconstriction and sudden death from acute right heart failure; and repeated embolization can raise pulmonary vascular resistance permanently (pulmonary hypertension).
## explicit_objective
State that the deep leg veins are the major source of pulmonary emboli, and connect this to the systemic-vein-to-lung course of thromboembolism and the size-dependent effects.
## pitfalls
Assigning left-heart mural thrombi or arterial-plaque thrombi as a major pulmonary-embolism source — these embolize down the systemic arterial tree instead, since the book's course-of-emboli rule sends only systemic-vein or right-heart emboli to the lungs.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-BA7D3F37AEFEFC
## label
Systemic emboli, mostly from the heart, commonly impact the lower extremities and CNS arteries
## canonical_key
embolism.systemic.sources-and-sites
## aliases
Systemic embolization sites
Sources of systemic emboli
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The department book lists the lower extremities and the CNS as the common sites of arterial embolization in systemic embolism, with the intestines, kidneys and spleen as less common targets. About 80% of systemic emboli originate from the heart — mural thrombi from myocardial infarcts or left atrial thrombosis — with the remainder from aortic aneurysms, thrombi overlying ulcerated atherosclerotic plaques, fragmented valvular vegetations, or paradoxical emboli from the venous system. Because these emboli travel out through the aorta and its branches to lodge in end arteries, the effect is usually infarction or limb gangrene.
## explicit_objective
State that systemic emboli commonly impact the lower extremities and CNS arteries, and connect this to their predominantly cardiac, arterial-side origin.
## pitfalls
Placing systemic emboli in veins (CNS or otherwise) — systemic emboli travel through the arterial tree from the left heart or aorta, so they lodge in arteries, not veins.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-0904A6DD216F4A
## label
Chronic ischemia is gradual, incomplete arterial occlusion caused by atherosclerosis, arteritis or compression
## canonical_key
ischemia.chronic.causes
## aliases
Causes of chronic ischemia
Acute vs chronic ischemia
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The department book classifies ischemia as acute (sudden, complete arterial occlusion, from thrombus/embolus, vessel strangulation, pedicle twisting, surgical ligature, arterial spasm, or extensive venous obstruction) or chronic (gradual, incomplete arterial occlusion). Chronic ischemia is caused by atherosclerosis, arteritis (such as marked endarteritis obliterans in syphilis), or artery compression by a tumour. Because the narrowing develops slowly, collaterals may form: with poor collaterals the result is ischemic atrophy and fibrous replacement (as in myocardial fibrosis from coronary atherosclerosis), while good collaterals produce no effect at all — in contrast to acute ischemia, where poor collaterals cause infarction or gangrene and good collaterals again produce no effect.
## explicit_objective
Attribute chronic ischemia to a gradual, incomplete arterial occlusion (atherosclerosis, arteritis, compression) and distinguish it from the sudden, complete occlusion of acute ischemia (thrombus, embolus, ligature, spasm).
## pitfalls
Classifying thrombus, embolus or surgical ligature as causes of chronic ischemia — the book places all three under acute ischemia, since each produces a sudden, complete occlusion rather than a gradual, incomplete one.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-116B0060AF7B6B
## label
Dry gangrene has a well-developed line of demarcation and line of separation, unlike moist gangrene
## canonical_key
gangrene.dry.demarcation-and-separation
## aliases
Dry vs moist gangrene
Line of demarcation and separation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Gangrene is massive tissue necrosis followed by putrefaction. Dry gangrene, the typical example being senile gangrene of the toes from severe atherosclerosis, is caused by occlusion of arterial blood supply alone, usually affects exposed limbs, and leaves the affected part dry, shrunken, mummified and black, with minimal bacterial growth because of the dryness; it shows a well-developed red line of demarcation (a zone of inflammation between gangrenous and viable tissue) and, later, a well-developed line of separation (a groove formed by granulation tissue and fibrosis) that can progress to natural amputation. This contrasts with moist gangrene, caused by occlusion of both arterial and venous supply, which usually affects internal organs, is stuffed with blood favouring rapid putrefactive bacterial growth, causes severe fatal toxemia, and has a poorly developed line of demarcation with no line of separation and no natural amputation.
## explicit_objective
Attribute a well-developed line of demarcation and line of separation to dry gangrene, and contrast this with the poorly demarcated, toxemic picture of moist gangrene.
## pitfalls
Assuming any form of gangrene shares dry gangrene's slow, well-demarcated course — moist gangrene, caused by combined arterial and venous occlusion, spreads rapidly with severe toxemia and no natural separation.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-EC6AE6777CAAC4
## label
Pale infarcts occur from arterial occlusion in solid organs with end-arterial circulation, unlike red infarcts
## canonical_key
infarction.types.pale-vs-red
## aliases
Pale vs red infarcts
Causes of pale infarction
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
An infarct is an area of ischemic necrosis from occlusion of the vascular supply, most commonly by arterial thrombosis or embolism. The department book gives arterial occlusion in solid organs with end-arterial circulations — the heart, spleen and kidney are its named examples — as the cause of pale infarcts, because there is no parallel or collateral supply to let blood re-enter the necrotic zone. Red infarcts, by contrast, occur with venous occlusions (such as ovarian torsion), in loose tissues (lung) or tissues with dual circulation (lung, small intestine) where a parallel supply can still perfuse the necrotic zone, or in tissues that were already congested before the occlusion, because sluggish venous outflow lets blood accumulate there.
## explicit_objective
Attribute pale infarcts to arterial occlusion in solid, end-arterial organs, and contrast this with the three causes of red infarction (venous occlusion, dual circulation, prior congestion).
## pitfalls
Assigning venous occlusion, dual circulation or prior congestion to pale infarcts — the book lists all three as causes of red, not pale, infarction, since each lets blood re-enter the necrotic zone.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-6E5B00C79396B9
## label
Septicemia is multiplying bacteria and toxins in the blood; pyemia is circulating septic emboli from septic thrombophlebitis
## canonical_key
sepsis.terminology.bacteremia-toxemia-septicemia-pyemia
## aliases
Bacteraemia vs toxemia vs septicemia vs pyemia
Blood-stream spread of infection
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
When local infection fails to stay contained, it spreads by the bloodstream in one of four patterns the department book keeps distinct. Bacteraemia is circulation of low-dose or low-virulence organisms, usually causing no disease unless there is a pre-existing abnormality such as diseased heart valves. Toxemia is organismal toxins in the circulation, from gram-negative endotoxin released on bacterial death, or gram-positive exotoxins acting at specific sites (tetanus, diphtheria, bacillary dysentery). Septicemia, usually fatal, is the circulation of large numbers of multiplying bacteria together with their toxins, from either a high-virulence organism or an ordinary infection in a low-resistance host, producing septic shock, serosal inflammation, splenic swelling, Waterhouse-Friderichsen adrenal necrosis, toxic organ injury and DIC. Pyemia is the circulation of septic emboli, arising from septic thrombophlebitis, which impact in small vessels to form pyemic abscesses (pulmonary, systemic or portal, depending on the venous route).
## explicit_objective
Distinguish bacteraemia, toxemia, septicemia and pyemia by mechanism (circulating low-virulence organisms; circulating toxin alone; multiplying bacteria plus toxin; circulating septic emboli) and typical outcome.
## pitfalls
Treating 'bacteria in the blood' as a single entity — the book separates whether it is few organisms causing little effect (bacteraemia), toxin without bulk organisms (toxemia), massive multiplying organisms with toxin (septicemia), or organized septic emboli (pyemia), each with a different source and consequence.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-4FF695608AFC33
## label
Primary tuberculosis develops at the lungs, intestine, tonsils or skin in an unsensitized host
## canonical_key
tuberculosis.primary.sites-of-first-infection
## aliases
Sites of primary tuberculosis
Primary tuberculous complex
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Primary tuberculosis develops in an unexposed, unsensitized person, from an exogenous source, and is characterized by the triad of a tuberculous tissue lesion, lymphangitis and lymphadenitis. The department book lists four sites: the lungs (by inhalation of droplets, forming the primary pulmonary complex of Gohn's focus, lymphangitis and hilar lymphadenitis); the intestine (by ingestion of self-swallowed sputum or unpasteurized bovine milk, forming the primary intestinal complex with tabes mesenterica); the tonsils (also by ingestion, mainly in children); and the skin (rarely, by inoculation, in laboratory workers).
## explicit_objective
List the four sites of primary tuberculosis and connect each to its mode of infection (inhalation for lung, ingestion for intestine and tonsil, inoculation for skin).
## pitfalls
Confusing primary tuberculosis's sites with secondary tuberculosis's — secondary disease reactivates or reinfects the same organ systems (apex of lung, terminal ileum) but with a different immune and morphological picture (extensive caseation, minimal lymph node involvement), not a new site list.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-B525A58C8119DE
## label
Chronic fibrocaseous pulmonary tuberculosis causes an apical cavity with acinar lesions but insignificant lymph node disease
## canonical_key
tuberculosis.secondary.chronic-fibrocaseous-pulmonary
## aliases
Secondary pulmonary tuberculosis
Apical cavitation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Secondary pulmonary tuberculosis, from reinfection or reactivation, starts at the lung apex (less blood supply, more aeration). With moderate bacilli and moderate immunity it produces chronic fibrocaseous pulmonary tuberculosis: a slow course over years, repeated attempts to encapsulate the lesion by fibrosis, significant caseation, and — because prior infection has already built immunity — insignificant lymph node lesions. Grossly it forms an apical cavity (caseation evacuated through a bronchus, at first irregular and yellow, later smoothed by fibrosis, enlarging as its wall is destroyed by caseation and reforms further out) and clover-leaf-shaped acinar lesions at the lung base from aspirated caseous material, which can confluence into confluent pneumonia. Complications include hemoptysis, pneumothorax, direct and blood spread, lung fibrosis with right heart failure, bronchiectasis, aspergilloma and secondary amyloidosis.
## explicit_objective
Describe chronic fibrocaseous (secondary) pulmonary tuberculosis: apical cavitation, acinar lesions, and insignificant lymph node involvement, contrasted with primary tuberculosis's prominent lymphadenitis.
## pitfalls
Assuming secondary tuberculosis reproduces primary tuberculosis's prominent lymph node triad — pre-existing immunity from the earlier primary infection is exactly what limits lymph node involvement in the secondary disease, per the book's own reasoning (migration inhibition factor keeps bacilli-laden macrophages from reaching the nodes in large numbers).

# Item
## id
CON-INF-420A4970289CE0
## label
The primary intestinal tuberculous complex presents mainly as tabes mesenterica, distinct from secondary intestinal tuberculosis's girdle ulcers
## canonical_key
tuberculosis.intestinal.primary-complex-and-tabes-mesenterica
## aliases
Tabes mesenterica
Primary vs secondary intestinal tuberculosis
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
The primary intestinal tuberculous complex forms a small intestinal tubercle in Peyer's patches of the terminal ileum, which may ulcerate, together with tuberculous lymphangitis and mesenteric draining lymph nodes that become enlarged, caseous and adherent — the finding the book names tabes mesenterica. Secondary intestinal tuberculosis, by contrast, occurs in adults from swallowed infected sputum or reactivation, producing multiple transverse (girdle) ulcers with undermined edges mainly at the terminal ileum and caecum, healing by fibrosis, with only minimal mesenteric node involvement; its complications include hemorrhage, fistulae, perforation, fibrous stenosis or obstruction, spread to the peritoneum, and secondary amyloidosis.
## explicit_objective
State that the primary intestinal complex presents mainly as tabes mesenterica (mesenteric lymphadenitis), and distinguish it from secondary intestinal tuberculosis's girdle ulcers with undermined edges.
## pitfalls
Assigning the girdle-shaped, undermined-edge ulcers to primary intestinal tuberculosis — the book describes these specifically for the secondary disease, where transverse lymphatic spread along the bowel wall produces the girdle pattern.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-9534D78BFE4A9C
## label
The hard chancre is the primary-stage lesion of syphilis; secondary-stage lesions (rash, condyloma latum, mucous patches) come later
## canonical_key
syphilis.primary.hard-chancre
## aliases
Primary syphilis
Hard chancre
Stages of syphilis
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Syphilis is an infective granuloma caused by Treponema pallidum. Its primary stage, the hard chancre, appears about two weeks after infection at the entry site (genital or extragenital); it is a single raised red papule that ulcerates into a rounded, superficial, painless but very infective ulcer with flat edges, clean floor and firm margin/base, healing by a thin atrophic scar, with painless, mobile, separately enlarged draining nodes. Microscopically the subepithelial tissue shows syphilitic granulation tissue (dense perivascular plasma cells, lymphocytes, occasional giant cells) with prominent early endarteritis obliterans. The secondary stage follows about two months later, with a generalized macular/papular/pustular skin rash, condyloma lata, scaly desquamation, mucous patches and generalized lymphadenitis; the tertiary stage, at about two years, produces localized gumma or diffuse syphilitic infiltration in organs such as liver, heart, testis, skull and tongue.
## explicit_objective
Identify the hard chancre as syphilis's primary-stage lesion, and place the skin rash, condyloma latum and mucous patches at the later secondary stage.
## pitfalls
Assigning secondary-stage lesions (rash, condyloma latum, mucous patches) to the primary stage — the book's own timeline places the chancre first (about two weeks), with these other lesions appearing roughly two months after it.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-C1BDF0DDE34498
## label
Nodular (lepromatous) leprosy shows vacuolated lepra cells and low immunity, unlike the non-caseating granuloma of tuberculoid leprosy
## canonical_key
leprosy.lepromatous.nodular-pattern
## aliases
Lepromatous leprosy
Lepra cells
Tuberculoid vs lepromatous leprosy
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Leprosy is an infective granuloma caused by Mycobacterium leprae, an acid-alcohol-fast bacillus, acquired by prolonged close contact and affecting skin and nerves. Its pattern depends on the host's TH1 response. Lepromatous (nodular) leprosy develops with low TH1 cell-mediated immunity: the granuloma is macrophages, lymphocytes, plasma cells and giant cells, and the macrophages engulf proliferating bacilli, becoming vacuolated lepra cells; skin nodules on the face give a leonine appearance, with nasal, nerve (ulnar, peroneal, facial — symmetric thickening) and trophic changes, and visceral spread. Tuberculoid leprosy, by contrast, develops with a strong TH1/interferon-gamma response and low bacillary burden, producing non-caseating granulomas of epithelioid cells, giant cells and lymphocytes around dermal nerve fibers, hypopigmented anesthetic skin macules, and asymmetric nerve thickening.
## explicit_objective
Attribute vacuolated lepra cells and low immunity to lepromatous (nodular) leprosy, and non-caseating granulomas with anesthetic patches and high immunity to tuberculoid leprosy.
## pitfalls
Attaching the non-caseating-granuloma / anesthetic-patch picture to lepromatous leprosy — the book assigns that combination, and the higher TH1 immunity that produces it, to the tuberculoid form instead.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-46951EC6118C17
## label
Actinomycosis is a chronic gram-positive bacterial disease with multiple sinuses discharging sulphur granules
## canonical_key
actinomycosis.features.sulphur-granules-and-sinuses
## aliases
Actinomyces israelii
Sulphur granules
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Actinomycosis is an infective granuloma caused by Actinomyces israelii, an anaerobic gram-positive commensal of the mouth, carious teeth and intestine that turns pathogenic endogenously when immunity is lowered. Grossly it forms multiple intercommunicating abscesses opening to the surface through multiple sinuses, discharging pus and yellow bacterial colonies (sulphur granules); microscopically each abscess has a central zone of colonies with club-like filament swellings, a mid zone of mixed inflammatory cells, and an outer fibrous zone. Cervicofacial disease (60%, entering via buccal mucosa trauma) is commonest, followed by intestinal (20%), pulmonary (15%) and skin (5%) forms; because the causative filaments are too large for lymphatics, spread when it occurs is by blood, not lymphatic channels.
## explicit_objective
Describe actinomycosis as a chronic, gram-positive bacterial disease with multiple draining sinuses and sulphur granules, distinct from a fungal infection.
## pitfalls
Classifying actinomycosis as a fungal disease because of its mycetoma-like clinical picture — the causative organism, Actinomyces israelii, is a gram-positive bacterium, and its lesion shows inflammatory-cell and fibrous zones rather than an epithelioid granuloma.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-324F03A30F7F5F
## label
AIDS's crisis phase brings opportunistic infection, the main cause of death, and characteristic malignancies including primary CNS lymphoma
## canonical_key
aids.crisis-phase.opportunistic-infection-and-neoplasms
## aliases
AIDS cause of death
AIDS-associated malignancies
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
HIV, a retrovirus causing selective loss of CD4+ helper T cells, progresses through an early acute phase, a middle latent chronic phase (low-level replication, possible generalized lymphadenopathy), and a final crisis phase once host defence breaks down and viral replication recrudesces. This crisis phase comprises opportunistic infections — protozoal/helminthic (strongyloidiasis, pneumocystis), fungal (candidiasis, histoplasmosis, cryptococcosis), bacterial (tuberculosis, atypical mycobacteriosis, salmonella, shigella) and viral (CMV, HSV, EBV) — which the department book names as the main cause of death, and malignant neoplasms: non-Hodgkin's lymphoma, Burkitt's lymphoma, primary CNS lymphoma and Kaposi's sarcoma.
## explicit_objective
State that opportunistic infection is the main cause of death in AIDS, and name the crisis-phase malignancies (non-Hodgkin's lymphoma, Burkitt's lymphoma, primary CNS lymphoma, Kaposi's sarcoma).
## pitfalls
Treating any HPV-related lesion (common or genital warts, cancer cervix) as one of the book's AIDS-associated malignancies — the book's own AIDS list names non-Hodgkin's/Burkitt's/primary CNS lymphoma and Kaposi's sarcoma specifically, and does not include HPV lesions in that list.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-92D5067ECA6A8D
## label
Hepatic bilharziasis preserves the liver lobules but fibroses the portal tracts, with angiomatoid channels bypassing the obstruction
## canonical_key
bilharziasis.hepatic.periportal-fibrosis
## aliases
Bilharzial periportal fibrosis
Pipe stem fibrosis
Hepatic bilharziasis
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Hepatic bilharziasis (bilharzial periportal fibrosis) is caused by Schistosoma mansoni ova carried as emboli from the colon through the portal vein to the portal tracts, where a granulomatous reaction heals by fibrosis. The liver is early enlarged from reticuloendothelial hyperplasia, but late, with progressive portal fibrosis, becomes shrunken, firm and irregular with a thickened capsule; the cut surface is dark brown from hematin taken up by Kupffer cells, with portal tracts widened into thick white bands (pipe-stem fibrosis). Liver cells and lobules are not themselves affected — only the portal tracts (fibrosis, ova granulomas, and angiomatoid formation: dilated collateral channels between hepatic artery and portal vein branches, bypassing the fibrotic obstruction) and sinusoids (hematin-laden Kupffer cells). Portal fibrosis and angiomatoid high-pressure shunting together produce portal hypertension: splenomegaly, ascites, esophageal varices, piles, caput medusae, portal vein thrombosis, and, if hepatitis is superimposed, ammonia encephalopathy.
## explicit_objective
Describe hepatic bilharzial periportal fibrosis: preserved liver lobules, portal tract fibrosis with angiomatoid channels, and the shift from early enlargement to late shrinkage.
## pitfalls
Assuming hepatic bilharziasis disturbs the liver parenchyma itself, or stays enlarged at a late stage, or produces jaundice from Kupffer-cell bile pigment — the book states the lobules are unaffected, the liver shrinks late, and the Kupffer-cell pigment is hematin, not bile.

# Item
## id
CON-INF-1A5FE3A93775E2
## label
Colonic bilharziasis causes hemorrhage, dysentery and hepatic spread via the portal vein, but the book does not name colon cancer among its complications
## canonical_key
bilharziasis.colonic.rectosigmoid-lesions
## aliases
Bilharzial colitis
Rectal bilharziasis
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Bilharziasis of the large intestine (bilharzial colitis) affects mainly the rectosigmoid segment, with the same lesion spectrum as elsewhere — sandy patches, bilharzial polyps (much more common here than at other sites) and bilharzial ulcers — plus, in whole-wall fibrosis, a bilharzioma (a tumour-like mass of ova deposited outside the colonic wall, with no ova reaching the lumen, so-called closed intestinal bilharziasis). Its complications are hemorrhage (fresh blood in stool, from the rectosigmoid location), secondary infection producing dysentery (diarrhea, blood, mucus, tenesmus), and, rarely, stenosis and obstruction (uncommon because the colon is wide and lesions are usually superficial). Because ova are carried from the colon as emboli through the portal vein to the liver, hepatic bilharziasis is a described route of spread from colonic infection.
## explicit_objective
List the complications of colonic bilharziasis (hemorrhage, dysentery, hepatic spread) and recognise that, unlike the urinary bladder, the book does not name colon cancer among them.
## pitfalls
Assuming colonic bilharziasis carries the same explicit precancerous designation the book gives bladder bilharziasis (cystitis glandularis, leukoplakia) — the book's colonic complications list stops at hemorrhage, dysentery and (rarely) obstruction, without naming colon cancer.

## id
CON-INF-6C300619B04AE3
## label
Pulmonary bilharziasis lesions are caused by both trapped ova and dead worms, reaching the lung mainly via Schistosoma hematobium's vesical route
## canonical_key
bilharziasis.pulmonary.ova-emboli-and-verminous-pneumonia
## aliases
Verminous pneumonia
Pulmonary bilharziasis
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Emboli of ova and worms reach the lung in about 20% of bilharzial cases, most often via Schistosoma hematobium: ova pass from the vesical plexus through the internal iliac veins and inferior vena cava to the lungs. Schistosoma mansoni ova reach the lung less directly, only in cases of bilharzial hepatosplenomegaly, via opened portal-systemic anastomoses. Once there, trapped ova cause endarteritis and interstitial fibrosis, while dead worms cause necrosis and severe allergic inflammation of lung tissue, termed verminous pneumonia — so both the ova and the worms themselves are named causes of the pulmonary lesions.
## explicit_objective
Attribute pulmonary bilharzial lesions to both trapped ova (endarteritis, fibrosis) and dead worms (verminous pneumonia), and identify the hematobium-dominant vesical-plexus route to the lung.
## pitfalls
Assuming pulmonary bilharziasis is a common complication of bilharzial disease generally, or that Schistosoma mansoni is its main cause, or that it can be complicated by squamous cell carcinoma — the book gives it a 20% incidence, attributes it mainly to S. hematobium via the vesical plexus, and does not describe an SCC complication (that is reserved for the bladder).

# Item
## id
CON-FND-615C9D19BED36F
## label
The Papanicolaou (PAP) stain is the perfect stain for cytology specimens
## canonical_key
cytology.papanicolaou-stain.cervico-vaginal-screening
## aliases
PAP stain
Cytopathology screening
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Cytopathology is a rapid, inexpensive, non-invasive method of patient evaluation, used for screening (cervico-vaginal PAP smears for premalignant cervical lesions, urine screening after industrial carcinogen exposure), initial diagnosis, and treatment follow-up. Samples include fluids (effusions, ascitic fluid, urine, CSF), smears, sputum, fine needle aspiration cytology and discharge; fluids are centrifuged and the sediment smeared like a blood film, then fixed in 95% ethanol. The department book states directly that the perfect stain for cytology is the Papanicolaou stain (PAP).
## explicit_objective
Name the Papanicolaou (PAP) stain as the standard cytology stain and state the main uses of cytopathology (screening, diagnosis, follow-up).
## pitfalls
Confusing the PAP stain with H&E (routine tissue histology), Alcian blue (a mucin cytohistochemical stain) or Prussian blue (an iron/hemosiderin stain) — the book reserves 'the perfect stain for cytology' specifically for PAP.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-85395A8500798E
## label
Total body irradiation causes four dose-graded acute syndromes -- radiation sickness, haemopoietic, gastrointestinal and cerebral
## canonical_key
radiation.total-body.acute-syndromes
## aliases
Acute radiation syndrome
Radiation sickness
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Ionizing radiation causes chromosome breakage and other injury; total body exposure produces early (acute) syndromes graded by dose: radiation sickness (50-200 rem — fatigue, anorexia, nausea, transient mild blood count drop), haemopoietic syndrome (200-600 rem — bone marrow depression), gastrointestinal syndrome (300-1000 rem — severe diarrhea, vomiting, GIT haemorrhage) and cerebral syndrome (over 1000 rem — convulsions and coma, lethal). Late effects, from low-dose accident survivors or slow cumulative exposure, include malignancy (e.g. leukemia), genetic disorders, and infertility or fibrotic complications; specific organs also show their own patterns (GIT ulceration/colitis, germ-cell destruction and sterility, ARDS then pulmonary fibrosis, pancytopenia or aplasia, lymph node atrophy, bone osteomyelitis/necrosis/osteosarcoma, nephritis, vasculitis).
## explicit_objective
Name the four dose-graded acute total-body-irradiation syndromes (radiation sickness, haemopoietic, gastrointestinal, cerebral) in order of increasing dose and severity.
## pitfalls
Inventing a 'vascular syndrome' as a fifth acute-exposure category — the book names radiation vasculitis and thrombosis only as one of the separate organ-specific late effects, not as one of the four graded whole-body dose syndromes.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-BFCED5D968B197
## label
Pellagra's 'three D's' -- dermatitis, diarrhea, dementia -- come from niacin deficiency, not vitamin D deficiency
## canonical_key
pellagra.niacin-deficiency.three-ds
## aliases
Niacin deficiency
Three D's of pellagra
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Niacin (vitamin B3) is formed from spare tryptophan; maize (corn) is low in tryptophan and its own niacin is poorly absorbed, so pellagra is endemic in corn-eating societies and in alcoholics. Pellagra produces the 'three D's': dermatitis (red, thick, scaly, sharply demarcated, irregularly pigmented skin in sun-exposed areas, with a beefy red tongue; microscopically acanthosis, confluent parakeratosis, hyperkeratosis and increased pigmentation); diarrhea (hyperaemic colon with pseudomembrane, necrosis and mononuclear infiltration); and dementia (mental illness, frontal-lobe neuron loss, demyelination of the lateral and posterior spinal columns — subacute combined degeneration); the book notes an unlisted fourth 'D', death. This is distinct from vitamin D deficiency (rickets/osteomalacia), which affects bone mineralization (bowing of long bones, rachitic rosary, craniotabes) rather than skin, gut and brain.
## explicit_objective
Name the 'three D's' of pellagra (dermatitis, diarrhea, dementia) as niacin deficiency's features, and distinguish them from vitamin D deficiency's bone changes (rickets/osteomalacia).
## pitfalls
Merging pellagra's clinical picture with rickets' — bowing of the long bones and other bone deformities belong to vitamin D deficiency, not to niacin deficiency, which the book keeps in a separate chapter with its own three-D mnemonic.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-960CB57A05C308
## label
Neostigmine reverses competitive neuromuscular block by anticholinesterase-driven acetylcholine accumulation at nicotinic receptors
## canonical_key
anticholinesterase.reversal.competitive-neuromuscular-block
## aliases
Neostigmine reversal mechanism
Anticholinesterase neuromuscular block reversal
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Neostigmine is an anticholinesterase that inhibits acetylcholinesterase, letting acetylcholine accumulate at nicotinic (Nm) receptors of the neuromuscular junction. This accumulated acetylcholine competes with, and displaces, a non-depolarizing (competitive) neuromuscular blocker from the receptor, relieving the block -- the same anticholinesterase mechanism neostigmine uses to diagnose and treat myasthenia gravis and manage post-operative ileus or urinary retention. Because it also raises acetylcholine at muscarinic sites, neostigmine is routinely co-administered with an antimuscarinic (atropine or glycopyrrolate) to prevent bradycardia.
## explicit_objective
Explain neostigmine's reversal of competitive neuromuscular block as anticholinesterase-driven acetylcholine accumulation outcompeting the blocker at nicotinic receptors.
## pitfalls
Attributing neostigmine's reversal to direct nicotinic agonism or CNS penetration -- it is an indirect, enzyme-inhibition mechanism, and as a quaternary amine it does not cross the blood-brain barrier appreciably.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-F9E1875546E70D
## label
M2 muscarinic receptors are Gi-coupled and decrease intracellular cAMP, slowing heart rate
## canonical_key
muscarinic.m2-receptor.cardiac-camp-signaling
## aliases
M2 receptor signal transduction
Gi-coupled receptors and cAMP
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
M2 muscarinic receptors, located in the heart, are Gi-protein coupled: their stimulation inhibits adenylate cyclase, decreasing intracellular cAMP, which produces the receptor's negative chronotropic (heart-rate-slowing) effect. This contrasts with Gs-coupled Beta2 receptors, whose stimulation activates adenylate cyclase and increases cAMP; Gq-coupled Alpha1 receptors, which signal through phospholipase C/IP3/DAG to raise intracellular calcium rather than through cAMP; and ligand-gated Nn (nicotinic) receptors, which produce fast depolarization directly without a second messenger at all.
## explicit_objective
Identify M2 muscarinic receptors as Gi-coupled, decreasing cAMP and slowing heart rate, distinguishing them from Gs-coupled Beta2, Gq-coupled Alpha1 and ligand-gated Nn receptors.
## pitfalls
Assuming every autonomic receptor works through cAMP -- Alpha1 signals through calcium via Gq, and Nn receptors are ligand-gated ion channels with no G-protein or second messenger involved at all.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-0D81DEF34D7EB9
## label
Pilocarpine is a direct muscarinic agonist used as a sialagogue for xerostomia and a miotic for glaucoma
## canonical_key
pilocarpine.therapeutic-uses.sialagogue-and-miotic
## aliases
Pilocarpine therapeutic uses
Cholinomimetic alkaloid uses
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Pilocarpine is a natural tertiary-amine cholinomimetic alkaloid and direct muscarinic agonist. Stimulating salivary-gland muscarinic receptors produces profuse watery secretion, making pilocarpine a sialagogue for xerostomia (dry mouth) from causes such as radiation or Sjogren's syndrome; stimulating the ciliary/iris muscarinic receptors makes it a miotic, and a first-line agent, for glaucoma, lowering intraocular pressure by opening the trabecular drainage angle -- the opposite of a mydriatic effect.
## explicit_objective
Name pilocarpine's therapeutic uses as a sialagogue for xerostomia and a miotic for glaucoma, and rule out mydriasis, hair-growth inhibition and parkinsonism therapy.
## pitfalls
Calling pilocarpine a mydriatic -- as a muscarinic agonist it is a miotic, constricting rather than dilating the pupil, the pharmacological opposite of a mydriatic.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-F966C99135DCA0
## label
Pralidoxime reactivates phosphorylated acetylcholinesterase in organophosphorus poisoning, before enzyme aging occurs
## canonical_key
pralidoxime.mechanism.cholinesterase-reactivation
## aliases
Oxime therapy
Cholinesterase reactivation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Pralidoxime is an oxime that reactivates the acetylcholinesterase enzyme organophosphorus compounds have phosphorylated (inhibited), restoring its ability to break down accumulated acetylcholine. It must be given early, before the phosphorylated enzyme undergoes "aging" (a chemical change, typically within 24-48 hours, that makes reactivation impossible). Management protocol for organophosphorus poisoning pairs pralidoxime's enzyme-reactivating action with atropine's competitive antimuscarinic blockade and a benzodiazepine for seizure control, given concurrently.
## explicit_objective
Explain pralidoxime's mechanism in organophosphorus poisoning as reactivation of phosphorylated acetylcholinesterase, distinct from atropine's antimuscarinic role and benzodiazepine's anticonvulsant role.
## pitfalls
Attributing pralidoxime's benefit to a sedative, antimuscarinic or anticonvulsant action -- its only mechanism is direct enzyme reactivation, and it must be given before the phosphorylated enzyme ages.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-22013B46637B40
## label
Atropine is contraindicated in glaucoma because pupillary dilation can precipitate angle closure
## canonical_key
atropine.contraindications.glaucoma
## aliases
Atropine contraindications
Angle-closure glaucoma risk
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Atropine's antimuscarinic blockade paralyzes the constrictor pupillae muscle, producing mydriasis; in a susceptible eye this can close the drainage angle of the anterior chamber, obstructing aqueous outflow and raising intraocular pressure, which can precipitate acute angle-closure glaucoma. This is why glaucoma is a strict contraindication, in contrast to heart block and organophosphorus poisoning, which are among atropine's own established clinical uses, and diarrhea, which is not a contraindication.
## explicit_objective
State that atropine is contraindicated in glaucoma, because pupillary dilation can precipitate angle closure, and is not contraindicated by heart block, organophosphorus poisoning or diarrhea.
## pitfalls
Confusing atropine's uses with its contraindications -- heart block and organophosphorus poisoning are indications for atropine, not reasons to avoid it.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-44519EC1408937
## label
Antimuscarinic eye drops cause cycloplegia (loss of accommodation) alongside mydriasis
## canonical_key
antimuscarinic.eye-drops.cycloplegia-and-iop
## aliases
Cycloplegia
Antimuscarinic ocular effects
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Antimuscarinic (parasympathetic depressant) eye drops paralyze the ciliary muscle -- cycloplegia -- abolishing near-vision accommodation, alongside pupillary dilation (mydriasis) and a potential rise in intraocular pressure. This is why agents such as atropine, homatropine, cyclopentolate and tropicamide are used before fundus or refractive examination, to hold the lens fixed in its far-vision shape while the pupil stays open; it is the opposite of the pupillary constriction and lowered IOP a muscarinic agonist such as pilocarpine produces.
## explicit_objective
State that antimuscarinic eye drops cause cycloplegia (loss of accommodation) alongside mydriasis, and rule out pupillary constriction, decreased IOP and bronchospasm.
## pitfalls
Assuming antimuscarinic eye drops lower intraocular pressure or constrict the pupil -- both are muscarinic-agonist effects, the pharmacological opposite of what an antimuscarinic produces.

# Item
## id
CON-FND-8047A5BFDEE7E3
## label
Hyoscine is more markedly CNS depressant/sedating than atropine, which instead tends toward CNS stimulation and tachycardia
## canonical_key
hyoscine.vs-atropine.cns-depressant-effect
## aliases
Hyoscine vs atropine CNS effects
Scopolamine sedation
## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## definition
Compared with atropine, hyoscine (scopolamine) crosses into the CNS more readily and produces marked sedation and amnesia at standard doses, which is why it is used as a premedication for its calming, amnestic effect and as a motion-sickness antiemetic, and is preferred over atropine when atropine's tendency to cause tachycardia would be undesirable. Atropine, by contrast, has minimal cardiac effect from hyoscine and instead reliably blocks vagal tone to raise heart rate, and shows biphasic CNS action -- stimulation (restlessness) before any depression -- rather than hyoscine's predominant sedation.
## explicit_objective
Contrast hyoscine's marked CNS depressant/sedative action with atropine's more prominent tachycardic, less sedating profile.
## pitfalls
Assuming hyoscine and atropine are interchangeable antimuscarinics -- their CNS and cardiovascular profiles diverge sharply, which is exactly why one or the other is preferred in different clinical situations (premedication/motion sickness vs heart block).
